Notice is hereby given that the Cottonwood Heights City Council will hold a Business Meeting
beginning at approximately 7:00 p.m., or soon thereafter, on Tuesday, June 16, 2026, in the
Cottonwood Heights City Council Chambers located at 2277 East Bengal Boulevard, Cottonwood
Heights, Utah. The public may remotely view the open po1iions of the meeting through live
broadcast by connecting to https ://ww"\-v .voutubc.com/(il)Cottonwoodl lcights/streams, the
city's YouTube channel.
Persons physically attending the meeting in the City Council Chambers may make verbal comments
to the City Council when invited to do so, after completing a speaker comment card. Citizen
comments may also be submitted in writing to the city recorder at citvrecorder(a)ch.utah.2:ov by
noon, on the meeting date. In the interest of time and those attending the meeting live, however,
submitted written comments will be entered into the record and distrihutcd to the City Council,
but will not be read at the public meeting.
Civility and decorum should be applied in all discussions and debates. Difficult questions,
tough challenges to a point of view, and criticism of ideas and infonnation are legitimate elements
of a free democracy in action. Public officials and the public arc encouraged to refrain from making
belligerent, personal, impertinent, slanderous, threatening, abusive, or disparaging comments.

9.0     ADJOURN CITY COUNCIL BUSINESS MEETING
                                       PUBLIC COMMENT PROCEDURE
     During each City Council business meeting (specifically excluding work sessions), there will be a period
     not to exceed 60 minutes for citizen comment. (Code of Ordinances 2.30.160 (B)). Any person wishing
     to comment on any item not otherwise on the agenda for public comment may address the City Council
     during the Public Comment perio<l. Any person wishing to comment during the Public Comment period
     shall request recognition by the Mayor and upon recognition, approach the microphone and stale !heir
     name, slate if they are a resident of Cottonwood Heights and either provide their address or their Council
     district; and then address the City Council. Any person wishing to comment shall limit their comments to
     no rnore than three (3) minutes unless rnorc or less time is specified by the Mayor. Citizen groups who arc
     present will be asked to appoint a spokesperson, who shall limit their comments to no more than five (5)
     minutes unless more or less time i~ specified by the Mayor. AH comments shall be directed to the City
     Council. No person addressing the City Council during the comment period shall be allowed to Gomment
     more than once <luring that comment period. Speakers should not expect any debate or dialogue with the
     Mayor, City CounGil or City Staff during the meeting.
     The City Council may choose to limit the amount of time allotted to public comment on a specific issue.
     In such cases, special procedures for determining who will be allowed to speak and the order of such
     speakers will be determined by the Council. In the interest of time and those attending the meeting live,
     submitted written comments will be entered into the record, distributed to the City Council but not read at
     the public meeting.

1.0     WELCOME - Mayor Gay Lynn Bennion.

Mayor Gay Lynn Bennion called the~Gity Council Work Session to order at 4:00 PM.

2.0     REVIEW OF BUSINESS MEETING AGENDA-Mayor Gav Lynn Bennion.

Mayor Bennion revicw_cg, the Meeting Agenda and_stated that-the Youth City Council would be
sworn in after the Pledg't( of Allegiance, followed by City Council Committee Reports and Citizen
Comment Period. Staf[reports would include the Public Works Report and Cottonwood Heights
Police Department Report.

There were six action items. Item 7. l was consideration of Ordinance 464 Enacting Code Chapter
9.63, Community Clean Energy Program. Council Member Hyland remarked that a lot of public
comment was expected and asked if an agreement could be reached regarding potential action
should the public comment period run longer than the allotted one hour. Mayor Bennion stated
that each citizen would have three minutes to speak, so 20 citizens could comment within the hour.
The State Legislature limits speakers to one minute. City Manager, Jared Gerber reported that the
amount of time for each speaker could be decreased at the Council's discretion. Council Member
Birrell suggested that Mayor Bennion review the number of comment cards and make that
determination during the meeting.

Council Member Birrell asked why the vote was changed from June 2 to May 19. Council Member
Newell clarified that May 19 was the original date, but it was pushed to June 2. Mayor Bennion
stated that she requested the move to June 2 because the planned article was not included in the
newsletter, and she believed it was a good idea to provide more notification and additional time.
It was also decided via email, not in a public meeting, not to have the FlashVote survey.

Council Member Hyland stated that the Council had been discussing Utah Renewable
Communities ("URC") since January. Due to how divisive the issue was, the majority wanted to

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026         Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     1
finalize the vote and move on to other important matters. She would be meeting with FlashVote
to discuss the membership panel. They had promised a representative sample, but the panel
c1mently was not representative, and it is important that survey results can be trusted. The City
Council discussed the matter via email and did not feel that what was presented in the newsletter
included all infom1ation about the URC. Because there was not enough time to address those
issues, they chose not to have an article. She found it disappointing that it had been misrepresented.

Council Member Birrell stated that if the public had not had the opportunity to understand the
complexity of URC, she did not understand why they would be given even less time to do so.
Council Member Hyland stated that she had never received more feedback on an issue. Many
people were involved, aware, and commenting on URC, and she did not believe that two more
weeks would change anything. Council Member Birrell stated that comments were five-to-one in
favor of the program, which showed that it was an extraordinary response. In her four years on
the Council, she had never seen the public so enlivened over an issue. She interpreted that as
something that the public deeply cares about and should be given the foll len!:,1h of time.

Council Member Newell stated that stating the responses were five-to-one in favor was a misnomer
based on the comments he had received. There was also a whole segment of the population that
did not know about URC. Council 'Member Birrell stated that the City Council had received
comments through the website in addition to the public comments hear<l at five public meetings.
Her calculations showed that comments were five-to-one in favor. Com1cil Member Newell stated
that his experience differed. Council Member Hyland stated that fonn letters from people who did
not identify as being from Cottonwood Heights should be discounted, and those comprised
approximately 40% of the comments she received. Many communities had already voted, so the
City was not unique in voting on May 19 instead of June 2.

Council Member Holton encouraged citizens he spoke with to send c01mnents, but many of them
did not frequently engage with City government or feel comfortable with that process. Each
Council Member had different experiences with their outreach that was not necessarily reflected
in the emails.

Item 7.2 was consideration of Ordinance 465 Amending Code Sections 19.75.050 and 19.76.030
Regarding Separate Utility Meters. Cow1cil Member Hyland asked if the ordinance was intended
to address separate metering for accessory dwelling units ("ADU"). Community and Economic
Development Director, Jim Spung, reported that it was a resident-initiated request to update code
to allow second utility meters for guesthouscs and detached ADUs. He reviewed photographs of
the applicant's property. The existing utility meter is on the opposite end of the property from a
proposed ADU, and both Rocky Mountain Power and Enb1idge Gas recommended that he install
a second meter. It would greatly reduce impacts to existing infrastructure and landscaping and
save tens of thousands of dollars. Gas lines also lose efficiency over such a distance. Other
property owners with detached structures had made similar requests in the past.

Staff drafted the proposed Text Amendment to allow a second meter in cases where it would make
sense. The property owner would be required to provide evidence that it is not reasonable to
connect to the primary meter and be responsible for all utility billing on the site. For guesthouses,
they would also be required to record an affidavit with Salt Lake County clarifying that it is a

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026            Approved: _ __ _ __

                                                     2
guesthouse, not an ADU or separate residence. The affidavit would transfer with the title to
subsequent owners.

Mr. Spung reported that other communities in Utah allow second meters. Salt Lake County and
Eagle Mountain allow them on detached ADUs, and Provo requires a second meter. Fannington
allows detached ADUs to be subdivided and sold separately from the home. He believed the Text
Amendment was a good compromise to provide flexibility in appropriate circumstances without
opening the door to misuse.

Council Member Hyland asked if a second utility meter was allowed for internal ADUs.
Mr. Spung reported that second meters of any kind were currently prohibited on single-family
properties. He did not research internal AD Us, hut it would be uncommon to have a second meter
because it is part of the home.

Council Member Birrell stated that since AQJJs are one of the City's three moderate-income
housing strategies, she appreciated that Staff w.as taking a deeper dive into accommodating them.
Mr. Spung stated that it would remove some burdens on property owners who wish to run power
and gas to detached structures.
                                         --
                                       µ.=..c

In response to a question raised by Council Member Hyland, Mr. Spung reported that the Planning
Commission initially recommended that separate meters be allowed on all detached ADUs
regardless of the circumstances. That was an option, but his recommendation was that the
pennissions be narrowe_d to only allow a separate meter if it is not reasonable to connect to the
primary meter. ColL,Il£!1 Member Hyland asked if it would require a Conditional Use Permit
("CUP"). Mr. Spudflfoonfirmed that detached ADUs require Planning Commission review and
approval. The utility meter was more applicable to the building permit but would not create more
work for Staff.

Item 7.4 was consideration of Ordinance 467 Granting a Cable TV System Franchise to Comcast
of Utah II, Inc. Mr. Gerber reported that Comcast had been operating in the City without a
franchise agreement but had tired of pEJ-ying the additional fees. In response to a question from
Council Member Hyland, it was clarified that they would now pay a franchise fee. Public Works
Director, Matt Shipp, stated that they would pay a general foe rather than pay every time they do
a cut. City Attorney, Shane Topham, reported tha.t the City originally offered them a franchise
agreement in 2016 or 2018, but they were not interested until recently. The agreement had the
same terms as CenturyLink, with some minor language changes.

ftem 7.5 was consideration of Resolution 2026-31 Approving an Agreement with H. W. Lochner,

3.0       REPORTS

          3.1   V nllcy Emergency Communications Center - Erin Downs and Amber
                Widdison.

Valley Emergency Communications Center ("VECC") Fire Communications Commander, Amber
Widdison, introduced herself and Police Communications Commander, Rosa Olivos. VECC
Executive Director, Ivan Whitaker, was unable to attend the meeting. He had been with the center
for 24 months and had 29 years of public safety experience in emergency dispatch and as an
emergency medical technician and emergency medical services director. He was also a director
at Priority Dispatch and vice president at MD Ally. Mr. Whitaker had brought a lot of experience,
insight, and innovation to VECC.                      -

The executive leadership team included Chief of Operations, Elyse Hagge1ty; Chief Financial
Officer, Tyson Montoya; and Chief of Professional Standards and Development, Shondra Young.
Ms. Haggerty began her career with Salt Lake City 911 dispatch and had been with VECC for five
years. Mr. Montoya had been with VECC for two years and provided a strong financial foundation
for the center. Ms. Young was bujJding a new training division to benefit both new hires and
seasoned employees. VECC was co'i~ 1itting resources to developing new dispatchers who would
fill similar executive roles during their s;areers.

Commw1ications Commanders included Emergency Communications Commander, Nicole Lopez
and Administrative Communications Commander, Danni Harris. Ms. Lopez was over all law
enforcement and was the liaison between VECC, police chiefs, and deputy chiefs. Ms. Widdison
was the liaison between VECC, fire chiefs, and deputy chiefs. Facilities Manager, Brice Rawlings
kept VECC infrastructure running. Technical Services Manager, Brad Morris, has worked with
pai1ner agencies and cities on technical infrastructure.

VECC receives approximately 600,000 non-emergency calls every year, as well as 250,000 911
calls and 250,000 administrative and towing calls. Their total call volume is approximately 1.1
million calls. They serve 22 police and fire agencies across 17 municipalities. In response to a
question raised by Council Member Hyland, Ms. Widdison stated that the call volume could he
attributed to population, which was growing drastically on the west side. The population is also
older and requires more services. VECC is govemed by a board of trustees and finance
committees. It was fom1ed in 1998 and had grown in that time. They now have 147 total
employees.

In 2025, Cottonwood Heights generated approximately 15,000 police calls. Combined UFA calls
totaled 32,500 across all cities they serve.

VECC was facing issues with:

      •   Population growth



Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026        Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     4
            o  Utah is the fastest-growing state in the United States, with a growth rate of 18.4%
               and a projected population of four million by 2032. That would increase call
               volume, staffing needs, and associated fiscal demands. Salt Lake County was
               growing at a much faster rate than Utah, Davis, and Weaver Counties.
   •    Citizens living longer
            o By 2035, 22.8% of adults in the VECC service area will be 65 years of age or order,
               and only 15.2% under the age of 18.
   •    The mental health crisis
            o Mental health concerns contributed to a large number of constant and re-addressing
               callers. The suicide rate was 20.1 per 100,000 community members, and VECC
               was not equipped to handle the current call volume.

Council Member Newell asked ifVECC works with mental health partners in the Salt Lake Valley
that callers can be referred to. Ms. Widdison stated that the VECC was operating under a process
that was created 20 years previously, but they were actively working with the Salt Lake County
Mental Health Task Force to divert calls that do not require a public service response to the Mobile
Crisis Outreach Team ("MCOT"). They were not currently utilizing resources like the Department
of Veterans Affairs and University of Utah Behavioral Center. They did not have a triage process
to identify a caller in crisis, so they depended on keywords in callers' statements to divert them to
better resources. Not all agencies have an embedded social worker or someone who can provide
that type of clinical support. They were worki~g on a program to help those community members
access the co1Tect resources, which would also take some pressure off of first responders.

Ms. Widdison continued reviewing VECC's current challenges.

    •   Low acuity calls

           o 24% ofcalls were low acuity callers who could be seen by a doctor' s office in three
               to five business days rather than being handled through an emergency 911 response.
           o 30% of calls were for basic life support. VECC was working on ways to better
               identify and res·pond to basic life support calls.
           o 20% of calls were critical like cardiac arrest, accidents, house fires, etc.
    •   The need for technological enhancements

            o Text to 911 was currently used, and they could now receive video calls.
              Dispatchers could provide callers with a link to upload video, and doing so provides
              the agency with tum-by-tum directions based on the phone's location. The video
              can also be shared live with responders.
            o AI upgrades including:
            o Translation now correctly identifies the caller's language and actively interprets
              while waiting for a live interpreter.
            o VECC was revamping their medical protocols and implementing AI technology to
              better address calls.




Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026           Approved: _ _ __ _ _

                                                     5
Over the next three years, VECC hoped to have solutions to population, call volume growth, and
mental health response, and to create a technology strategy to bling the center into the next five
years and prepare them for the Olympics.

Mayor Bennion stated that some City Council Members had been through the Citizens Police
Academy, and it was very helpful to sec what the VECC was doing with technology.

In response to a question raised by Council Member Bin-ell, Ms. Widdison stated that the AI
technology can translate up to 36 languages, many of which dispatchers cannot identify just by
hearing it spoken. VECC has many dispatchers who can interpret Spanish, but a lot of callers
speak Farsi, Vietnamese, Korean, and other lm1guages. Ms. Olivos stated that it can be difficult to
identify the language a caller is speaking because dialects can be very different, but AI is able to
identify and begin translating the language.

Mr. Shipp reported that the Business Meeting agenda would include a resolution for approval of a

Butler Hills Drive and 2160 East intersections. Safety improvements for the crosswalk at Brighton
High School were also included in the design. City Engineer, Ben Nelsen, Council Member

and they had a lot of questions an<l ideas. Students, parents, and teachers spoke at the last City
Council Meeting about their concerns with the crosswalk.

The Bengal Boulevard project included two federal aid projects that were funded in 2022 or 2023.
An environmental assessment and 30% design for the intersections and trail p01tion were
completed by the City prior to receiving funding. A total of $1.1 million had been received from
the County for the majority of the trail, as well as traffic calming and pedestrian safety measures
throughout the corridor. The City was originally required to spend the funds by 2026, but Staff
requested and received an extension to December 2028. The City Council would be reviewing the
agreement during the Business meeting and making a decision on whether to move forward.

The remainder of the project was ready to move forward under the federal aid agreement. The
funds had been budgeted to pay the City's 6.77% portion of the overall cost, which was
approximately $300,000. The State, which administers federal aid funds, had contacted Mr. Shipp
several times to discuss the project timeline. Staff had met with the Wasatch Front Regional
Council ("WFRC"), which also questioned the timeline. Mr. Shipp info1med them that the project
was on hold until the Council determined whether to move forward. The 30% design was
completed several years previously. lt was then brought before the eunent City Council but stall cd
due to Council concerns. Mr. Shipp believed it was important to complete the entire project rather
than only the county portion as it would address traffic calming, pedestrian safety, bicycle safety,
and traffic flow.

       4.2        Fireworks Map Discussion - Battalion Chief Riley Pilgrim.

UFA Battalion Chief, Riley Pilgrim, reported that there was a push to restrict fireworks east of
1300 East in 2020 or 2021, and all cities from Salt Lake City to Draper followed that
recommendation. In 2022 and 2023, cities like Cottonwood Heights began to feel like it was
overly restrictive and started walking the restrictions back. The fireworks map had since been
fine-tuned based on legislative requirements.

Per Utah Code § l SA-5-202.5-l (b), fireworks can be restricted in:

        1. l. 1   Mountainous, brush-covered, forest-covered, or dry grass areas;
        1.1.2     Within 200 feet of waterways, trails, canyons, washes, ravines, or similar areas;
        1.1.3     Wildland Urban Interface areas (any urban interface areas where wild or
                  undeveloped area commingles with strnctures);
        l .1.4    A limited area outside the hazardous areas described above to facilitate a readily
                  identifiable closed area.

Chief Pilgrim reported that it was challenging to update the map to address areas without straight
roads, but they had done their best to incorporate the areas within statute and 200 feet beyond. A
map with straight lines helps residents understand where fireworks are allowed, and clearly
delineated areas also aids in enforcement.

The existing and proposed fireworks restrictions map was reviewed. Areas in red were already
restricted, and areas in purple were suggested after extensive review of geographic information
system ("DIS") data, citizen and Council feedback, and in-person visits to problem areas. Most
of the proposed changes were in challenging areas of the City that have many hillsides and ravines
adjacent to homes and no straight lines. These areas included Highland Avenue south of Bengal
Boulevard a!'!_d Parkridge Drive and the open space adjacent to Crestwood Park.

Chief Pilgrim reported that they bad to be careful not to create islands where residents can still
light fireworks because it would make enforcement difficult. They could use the provisions of
Item 1.1.4 above to restrict fireworks in the entire area, but that could be overly restrictive. For
example, the proposed new restricted area along Mill Lane included a pocket of approximately 35
houses that could technically be allowed to have fireworks, but no one else around tbcm could.
Sandy had restricted fireworks along Creek Road, and he did not want to create a situation where
they were allowed by Cottonwood Heights in the same area and a fire breaks out on a property in
Sandy.

Council Member Holton stated that he would prefer some flexibility to do sparklers with your
children, for example. However, he understood the reasons behind the restrictions. Chief Pilgrim
stated that the fire service met with representatives of the fireworks industry who were frnstrated
with the restrictions. They understood the public safety concerns, but tbe restrictions prohibit
people from using basic fireworks as well.

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for Moy 19, 2026           Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     11
Chief Pilgrim pointed out other unrestricted pockets in the City that were outside of the allowed
200 feet. However, if they were entertaining one resident's concerns, they should be willing to
entertain all of them, and people had expressed fire safety concerns about those areas. He and Mr.
Topham believed that Bengal Boulevard could he used as a clear cutoff. It extended slightly
beyond the hazard area, hut the statute allows for such exceptions to facilitate a readily identifiahle
dosed area. Chief Pilgrim then indicated other areas on the map that he would like to modify to
ensure that all restricted areas were clearly identifiable. Incorporating those limited areas outside
of the hazard zones would simplify enforcement and reduce additional threats to those areas, but
may be considered excessive by some residents. Once the map was approved, he would forward
it to Salt Lake County for inclusion on their website.

Mayor Bennion stated that Utah was in an extreme dro]._ght. The recommendations were serious

an education analysis of likely conditions. Areas outside of the red zone were not really impacted

        4.3     Budget Discussion - Finance Director Scott Jurges.

Finance Director, Scott Jurges, reviewed a General Fund Balance Analysis from FY2018-2019 to
the FY2026-2027 Tentative Budget. He analyzed stmiing and ending fund balances, as well as
the required 6% minimum balance and compensated absence reserve. The compensated absence
reserve had been very stable, at approximately 9% each year. The mialysis also included the
percentages of fund balance and compensated absences.

In FY2018-2019, the fund balance was 19% of revenues. For two years begiuning in FY2020-
202l, the City experienced extreme sales tax growth in addition to an influx of one-time funds that
increased the ratio to 30%. It then began to decline. In FY2023-2024, there a significant decline
of approximately $ 1.8 million. FY2024-2025 saw an approximately $1.7 million decline. The
current year was projected to have a decline of approximately $1. 9 million. The total ending fund
balance for the year was projected at $4 million, which was in line with FY2018-2029 but reflected
a percentage decrease from 19% to 14%. The FY2026-2027 Tentative Budget reflected a $220,000
increase in fund balance, which would maintain a percentage of 13% to 14% at the end of the year.
From a percentage standpoint, the City's financial status was worse than FY2018-2019 when the

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026            Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     14
last tax increase was implemented. With the proposed $3.5 million tax increase, base revenues
would be approximately $32.3 million. It could be seen as either increasing or decreasing the fund
balance, depending on whether it was viewed on a dollar or percentage basis.

Mr. Jurgcs reported that the minimum allowed fund balance percentage is 6%, and it was not
anticipated that l 00% of the Compensated Absences Reserve would be paid out in a given year.
Most property tax revenue is received between November and January, and sales tax revenue is
received on a two-month delay. A fund balm1ce is required to cover both ongoing expenses and
unexpected large capital expenditures. The larger the percentage, the better. Up to 35% is allowed,
and his preference was between 15% and 25%.

In response to a question raised by Council Member Hyland, Mr. Jurges clarified that the
percentage is of revenues, not expenditures, and excludes other revenues such as transfers in and
asset sales. Council Member Hyland stated that some general revenues arc received that arc
transferred out. Mr. Jurges confirmed that approximately $4 million of general revenues are
transferred out for debt service and $2 million far capital projects.

Mr. Jurges reported that there had been significant citing of Holladay's budget policies, and
Cottonwood Heights also included budget policies in past-year budget documents. He combined
both policy sources and added descriRtors and targets to create a seven-page draft policy document
with the goal of condensing it into a one-page summary to include with the Final Budget.

The document began with General Fund balance policies, as the balance fluctuates, but other funds
rely on the General Fund. Fund balance categories include non-spendable, restricted, committed,
assigned, and unassigqed. Unassigned fund balance is the balance that is truly available for
appropriation. Some poUcy elements were driven by Utah Code, and in those cases, the relevant
code section was cited. Those driven by the Cottonwood Heights Municipal Code did not yet have
citations.

If the goal is to maintain a General Fund balance of 15% to 25%, that becomes the target range,
with caution being needed to bring anything outside of it back within the target. A balance outside
of range would require City CQuncil consent. Once reserves arc used, they should be replenished
in a timely manner through a reduction in expenditures or an increase in revenues.

Other fund policies were reviewed. Mr. Jurgcs reported that the Capital Projects Fw1d and some
other funds typically do not typically need to carry a large balance. However, grants are often
funded three to five years after being awarded, and one proposed policy would set aside the
projected City match to avoid needing to appropriate monies from the General Fund.

Council Member Hyland remarked that a five-year budget had been requested as it would better
anticipate those needs and prevent them from being lost from Council to Council. She was
concerned about setting money aside for three to fom years when it could be included in the five-
year plan and more easily accOlmted for each budget cycle. Mr. Jurges stated that any additional
monies could be assigned within the Capital Projects Fund, and that infonnation could be disclosed
so it is clear to Staff and the Council that was set aside for a specific project match. It could also
be retained within the General Fund, but he bad concerns about including it with operational funds.

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026            Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     15
The set-aside would not preclude the City from pulling the money back into the General Fund if
the need arose. Council Member Hyland stated that operational dollars should be used for
operations if the City anticipated having the revenue to cover the match within three to four years.
Mr. Jurges stated that it could be set up in myriad ways, and any transfers would come before the
Council either as pait of the initial budget or an adjustment.

Mr. Jurgcs reported that the Stom1 Water Fund typically needs to maintain a balance of $500,000
in case of emergency repairs. Council Member Hyland asked why a dollar amount was specified
rather than a percentage. Mr. Jurges stated that they could consider a percentage. The dedicated
revenue source was approximately $2 million, so it would need to be set at 25%.

The Debt Service Fund typically does not can'Y a high balance, but it docs need approximately
$400,000 to $500,000 to cover deposits related to debt payment instrument requirements. The
paying agent must be prepaid approximately six months p1ior to the bond payment.

The Community Development and Renewal ("CORA") should carry a small balance for
redevelopment reasons. Any tax-increment financing development may require a higher balance.

The revenue policies specifies that Qne-time revenues should go toward one-time expenditures or
possibly to recoup fund balance if allowed by the revenue source. One-time revenues are often
required to be used for specific purposes, but the State and Local Fiscal Recovery Funds had no
such restrictions. If this policy was put in place, it would clearly match expenses to revenues to
make decisions easier if the revenues were depleted. For example, in the past the City received
seed grants to cover first-year costs for new police officers with the expectation that the City would
absorb those costs incfuture years.

Mr. Jurgcs stated that the City had been good at measuring revenue. For example, the City did not
initially hudgct $400,000 in the current year because they were not certain of the source and
amou' i-It was added lat~r in the year once the amount was clear.

Regardi11g expenditures, the policy would encourage the City to ensure that expenses do not exceed
current revenues and be as efficient as possible.

Compensation policies were hascd on cunent Holladay policies. If the City's salat)' range is more
than 5% below market, the grade would be adjusted upward. The City's pay plan should also be
reviewed and adopted annually with the budget.

Council Member Hyland noted that Item B states that the City should contract prior to considering
adding pennanent staff. Mr. Jurges stated that the policy was taken from Holladay but the language
could be modified at the Council's discretion. It can be good to outsource new positions if they
arc uncertain if the position will be long-tem1, but it often makes sense to bring in pcnnanent staff
when expanding something the City is already doing. However, an analysis of the benefits of in-
house versus contracted staff would be helpful in making that decision.

5.0     CITIZEN COMMENTS

Mayor Bennion opened the Citizen Comment Period and asked that comments be civil and kept to
one-and-a-half minutes or less. Members of the City Council had differing views on many topics,
but the win she sees every day is that they continue to work together and share experiences and
infonnation with one another so the Council can be prodnctive and not divisive in any way.

Kim Sleenblik stated that she is a resident of District 3 and is against the Utah Renewable
Communities ("URC") opt-out program. We can all do our part by opting in to the Rocky
Mountain Power Blue Sky program or other programs or installing solar panels, but there is a
difference between opt-in and opt-out. If you are relying on people to opt out, that is not good,
especially with the tax increases that we are having.

Dru Tidwell stated that she is a resident of District 2 and has lived in Cottonwood Heights for 21
years. She had stated her support for the URC before, and she attended two highly informative
meetings including the town hall and one at a neighbor's home where a group of approximately 15
citizens gathered to learn and advocate for the program. She had also done significant research
online to compare different energy sources and their costs and found that renewables are often half
as expensive as oil, gas, and nuclear~, Utah is in a unique place geographically to add solar, wind,
and geothermal to its energy portfolio. URC is a well-formed program, a collaboration of
government leaders from opposite sides of the political spectrum but came together thoughtfully
and in earnest to provide a platform where communities can work together to support sustainable
energy on a small scale. It was dedicated to grnw and would ve1y likely bring electricity bills
down within a few short years. The program was a kickstart to enliven good work for less
expensive and less harmful production of eleclric power. She urged the City Council to vote yes
to URC and make something historic happen in a very significant way.

Rob Jacobs stated that he was taken aback that there was even a question about whether to be in
or out._ Six years ago, Cottonwood Heights put $20,000 into the effort, and every mayor had
supported the program. The City Council passed a resolution that we should be 100% renewable
by 2030, and URC was the only thing he had seen that would make that happen. A survey indicated
that 88% of respondents were in favor of renewable energy, which was pretty significant. In his
opinion, URC was as good of a plan as they were ever going to get. It would allow him to power
his home 100% with renewable energy without installing solar panels, which would cost thousands
of dollars. Somebody mentioned that you could just subscribe to Blue Sky, but that was ridiculous
as it was four to five times the cost of URC. This program would force Rocky Mow1tain Power
into renewable energy. If the City opted out, that meant only the people who wanted to opt-out
would have a choice. If they opted in, everybody had a choice. His choice is to opt-in.

Leslie Kovach stated that she had lived in the area since 1984. In that time, she had watched the

to a more progressive government than was offered by Salt Lake County at the time. She felt that
our City would look out for the health of our citizens, and we would have a more responsive
Council when concerns and issues were raised by citizens. She feared that the Council may remove
Cottonwood Heights from the Community Clean Energy program. As a progressive community,
she endorsed the City staying in the program and continuing to move forward in funding it. For

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for Moy 19, 2026          App roved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     25
Every single default in civic and public life asks something of somebody, and we do not organize
public life around the principle that the default must always favor inaction. We organize it around
what best serves the community. 88% ofUtahns consider poor air quality a serious problem, 75%
are concerned about the state's water situation, and 71 % believe climate change is happening. That
is the Council's constituency and the majority they were elected to represent. If they voted no
because the opt-out was too much to ask of adults, what they were actually voting for was
stmcturing public policy to protect the passive preferences of the few over the active values of the
many. She had heard the objection that people should not be enrolled without consent, but they
were already enrolled in sewer, trash, schools, water infrastructure, grid maintenance, etc., often
without their vote. Social contract of community has always included shared systems. The URC
program gives everyone maximum choice. When a procedural objection consistently lands in the
direction that benefits the gas industry over public interest, it always results in more gas and less
renewables. At some point, citizens have to ask whether the procedure is really the concern.

Shelly Hanson read a statement from Mayor Mike Wickers, who was unable to attend. "I am sorry
I am not able to he here with you today. Along with former Mayors Cullimore and Peterson, I
would like to add my voice in opposition to the URC program with Rocky Mountain Power. I
have never met a person that does not want cleaner air, and I believe that renewable energy sources
can help decrease the amount of carbon monoxide in our atmosphere, but this program is relying
on an ill-conceived approach that flies in the face of fairness. The Federal Trade Commission is
an important government agency tasked with protecting consumers from illegal business practices
and can refer charges to the DOJ or implement fines for businesses that break our laws. If you go
to their website, the number-one common deceptive practice is opting in customers to the product
without their full and clear consent. It is remarkable thaj: the City of Cottonwood Heights is
considering implementing this exact practice on its residents with the justification that a
notification will be mailed to each household while knowing full well that a high majority will

           o The Staker Way drainage project design_0Yas completed, and the full project should
              be completed in Summer 2026.
           o Waterway improvements were ongoing at problem bus stops, a crosswalk on 2700

              presented in June. The project would extend from Bengal Boulevard to
              approximately Intersta!e 215.

              contract would be presented at the next meeting.
           o Mill and overlay on Fort Union Boulevard was part of the Cottonwood connection
              project and would extend from Nutree West to 3000 East. This was a maintenance
              project, not reconstruction. Pedestrian improvements in the area would be
              considered in 2027.

        7.1     Consideration of Ordinance 464 Enacting Code Chapter 9.63, Community
                Clean Energy Program (This ordinance will amend tlte citv code by enacting
                new Chapter 9.63 causing the city's-to participate in the Community Clean
                Energy Program under the Community Clean Energv Act, UTAH CODE ANN.
                54-17-901 to -909).

Council Member Holton stated that there was a lot of discussion ah out how not acting in a certain
way means you do not have compassion or care. A resident who lives on La Cresta D1ive reached
out to him to discuss water rates anci__issues she was having paying her bill. He visited her home,
which was run down. The door was·kept closed with duct tape. Many people say they can afford
the URC fee, but that is not always the case. He believes in renewables and is grateful to have
been part of the team that brought Tier 3 fuels to Utah for the first time, which reduce emissions
from vehicles manufactured after 2017 by 80%.

He told the residentt:fba [ the URC would he a $4 charge per month and she would be able to opt
out, but she said that sffe is on a fixed income and has to remove things from her grocery cart that
cost less than $4 because she cannot afford them. He told her that she would get a notice, but she
said that she \Vas \vcaring an N95 mask because her husband just finished cancer treatment, and
they w~e barely survivi11g to open_the notices they received for surgeries they could not afford
and did not have the wherewithal to keep up with tax notices or anything that is going on.

He shared another experience of visiting a family member who immigrated to Holladay from South
Korea. He asked the family member to open all his bills and explain what they mean to him, as
well as the action he hclicvcd he could take. He coul<l not communicate more than the dollar
amount owed and where it should he sent.

Council Member Holton believes that the program choice is a false one and URC is not an
equitable program, as an unfair structure was put in place and marketing was prioritized over
equity. People in District 1 with large energy consumption, multiple electric vehicles, and ample
flexibility in their income would pay the same fee as someone who lives in an apartment and can
barely put food on the table and is dreaming of saving enough money to purchase a home. He did
not take government action lightly, and it was very hard to make decisions that will impact
someone's pocketbook. He would vote no for those reasons.

He repo11ed that he received confirmation that a bill would he filed with the State legislature to
make the URC an opt-in program. Public policy is iterative. Many people had asked him to please

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026          Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     39
allow them the choice because voting no would remove their choice, and he hoped that they would
also embrace true choice. ff the program was opt in, he would do everything he could to ensure
that the current vote was not the end. He wanted to allow individuals who can afford it to actively
choose to take action, but otherwise he was unable to support the program.

Council Member Hyland stated that the program had been discussed at Council meetings since
Janumy, so anyone saying it had not been brought to them was not paying attention, which was
their concern with the opt out. Lack of attention was a serious issue. The Council was given four
days' notice of the URC town hall that they had been accused of not supporting. She could not
attend with short notice, but as it was the Mayor's town hall they also did not crash the party.

Residents mentioned conducting a FlashVote survey. Council Member Hyland brought FlashVote
to the City, and the company's promise was that they-would develop a member panel that was
representative of the community. However, it does not 1·epresent the community, and any results
would be biased. She would be speaking with them later in the week to discuss how to resolve the
issue.

Regarding the newsletter, sbc reported that the article was not sent to the Council until too late to
be able to make changes. She did not believe the newsletter should be used for political debate.
They were concerned that it was not a balanced article, which could have been remedied if they
had had more time.

Council Member Hyland stated that she appreciates thoughtful public engagement, and she had
heard from those who.:cwere both passionately -suppo1tive of m1d opposed the URC. She thanked
citizens for being resp$c_fful. She had done extensive research on the program, including listening
to the Public Service Commission meetings. At their December meeting, a commissioner said that
he had been a member for 40 years and they always heard from the passionate, but they also
represent those who are less passionate. There were 33,000 residents who were not present at the
City C~uncil meeting and may be less passionate about the topic, but she felt obligated to represent
them.

Clean air and reduced dependence on fossil fuels are common-sense goals. The intent of the
program was good, but citizens should expect their representatives to reach beyond the feel-good
sound bites to understand the structure of the legislation and how it will impact citizens, which is
what she spent countless hours doing. Her vote was not a statement against good intentions but
must reflect the structure of the program and any inherent 1isk to the City and residents.

The opt-out mechanism was not real choice and was chosen because the math of opt-in was not
convenient. She could not imagine establishing the precedent that a government entity can vote
to involuntarily subscribe its residents to a product or service. The opt-out model exploits human
frailties to not notice or fail to act by forgetfulness, inattention, or indifference, and the precedence
mattered. It was government overreach bordering on coercion. Opt-in is real freedom because it
recognizes a consumer's right to choose to act and not to be acted upon. If the organizers of the
clean energy program were so confident in citizen supp01t, it should have been structured as an
opt-in. Approximately 240 Utah cities and towns and 26 counties saw the program structure and
rejected it years ago. When it is opt-in, she will be all in.

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026              Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                     40
Another major concern was that there was no offramp for communities to exit URC if the program
did not deliver. If the costs escalate and benefits decline, the City Council cannot withdraw the
City to protect residents, which is bad governance. Council Member Hyland understood that the
City would have a vote, but she did not support the voting structured implemented by the URC
because it either gives a town like Springvale the same voting power as Cottonwood Heights or,
in certain circumstances, gives Salt Lake City too much power. The program burden is on the
residential customer ·with no guarantees. URC has projected that 20% of residential, 70% of
commercial, and 90% of industrial would opt out because of the higher charges to businesses. She
heard from a general manager of a ski resort who estimated their annual contribution as between
$50,000 and $70,000, which he does not believe his shareholders will support as they will want to
put those dollars into more predictable sustainability e' orts.

URC does not provide a direct energy benefit to cities or-citizens, and dollars could go to economic
development in Wyoming or Idaho as the proj_e{;ts do not have to be in Utah. Dollars are not
restricted to renewables and could go to nuclear or other energy-efficient and sustainable
technologies such as carbon capture of fossil foels. The regressive fee for residents was not fair,
and there was no requirement to mark it as optional on utility bills. Supporters note that future
participation is optional with a punitive $30 cancellation fee, but no one will know that because it
is not marked as optional in the future.

Council Member Hyland stated that Utah was adding a significant amount of solar capacity. Steve
Handy, who was the sponsor of the original 2019 bill, wrote an op-ed stating that since 2015,
approximately 94% of new c1ectric generating capacity added in Utah has been solar. That is not
because of a government mandate but because the market is speaking and solar is competitive. He
also sent her the book Inflection Point, which says, "We do not need government subsidies
anymore. We have reacbea a tipping point. We are going to see more renewables." Earlier that
day, NPR said in its news feed that roughly 22% of Utah's electricity comes from renewahles such
as wind, solar, and hydropower. The air also gets cleaner every year. The United States
Environmental Protection Agency recently announced that Salt Lake City and Provo areas have
met then 2006 PM2.5 24-hour national ambient air quality standard targets and are now in
attainment after 15 years, which underscores all the hard work Utah had done over the past decades
to improve air quality and demonstrates that Utah can meet federal air quality standards in a way
that respects local pri01itics and fosters cooperative federalism. She believes the focus sbou\d be
on those gains and not fear-mongering headlines that people tend to gravitate toward.

Council Member Newell stated that he is very concerned that people think the Council is not
concerned about clean air, because they are. He does a lot to conserve water. He docs not have a
sprinkler system. He gardens and has a lawn, but manages them conservatively. He is
conservative in how his family travels by not driving all over and picking up his grandchildren as
a group.

He was concerned about the equity issue and people who cannot afford to pay the bill. He spoke
with a young woman who pays $1,350 per month for a one-bedroom apartment on Fo1t Union
Boulevard. She works two jobs and can barely pay her bills, but the City could add a fee that she
did not even know existed. There were probably thousands of people in the same situation. If it

was an opt-in program, he would be all in and would also opt in. It was an issue because of how

Council Member Newell stated that they were not saying it was over but rather that it was not the

Council Member Birrell stated that she wished her Council colleagues had given as much scrutiny
to the budget over the past two to four years, as well as other things in the City. A number of

sustainability manager was doing and was told that she attended a monthly meeting with Rocky
Mountain Power for the ~Renewablc Co1mnunities Program. Approximately two weeks later, the
title changed and she became a planner/business license official/sustainability analyst.

Nineteen co1mmmities throughout Utah hag come together every month for the past seven years
to put together a program that would make systemic difference. She believes it is great that Council
Member Newell and many others conserve water when they water their gardens. However, when
surveyed, people say that core health comes from clean air, clean water, and she believes soil as
well. The opportunity to make this choice was historic. She ha<l never seen anything like it
advanced in Utah in a meaningful way. It was not small measures but the idea of systemically
moving forward as a state.

According to the International Renewable Energy Agency, in 2024 over 90% of new electricity
generating capacity came from renewable sources. Countries that do not have the same level of
affluence as the United States arc choosing renewables. The inflection point was reached a year
ago and will continue. The hook Council Member Hyland referred to also states that when
govermnent policy pushes back an<l docs not usher in renewables, it can put them 10 years behind
schedule. So many people wanted renewablcs to move ahead in 2010 or 2015, and now in 2026

they had the chance for those who choose to stay in the program to be completely cm-hon neutral
by 2030. That is a step in the right direction.

Mayor Bennion stated that the program had been studied and worked on for seven years. It was
passed with bipartisan support, with 23 of29 senators voting in favor. It took seven years because
it was carefully thought mtt. Out of the $4 each month, $0.12 goes to cover the fee fi.lr low-income
households, which will never have to pay to opt out. The goal is that as people are educated about
the program, more people will learn that they qualify for those benefits.

The program aims to have renewable energy by 2030. Nuclear and geothermal do not happen in
such a short timefrarn_e-and are out of the program's price range, so it is limited to solar and wind.
The program was movmg forward and could not he pulled back to change it to an opt-in program.
A total of 83% of commu nities in-the program had already voted to stay in, and it would move

Regarding the newsletter article that was not puhlished, it \.Vas written by the staff member who
had been attending monthly URC meetings since he hcgan working with the City and had read
minutes from p1ior meetings. She serves on the URC board, and in the past, the City representative
failed to attend those meetings. Mayor Bennion believed that the article was informative, and she
was sorry that they were unable to share it with the community due to a majority Council decision.

Saudi Arabia's Vision 2030 plan goal was for 50% renewable electrical power. The reason Utah
was not on the same path was that Rocky Mountain Power does not have it in its plan to add
renewable energy, partly due to the legislature. URC is a program that allows its 19 member
communities to have a say in the energy that is delivered to them.

When Salt Lake City made its Olympics bid several years ago, it passed a joint resolution
addressing climate change that included the following words: "The Community Renewable Energy
Program, formed following the passage of the aforementioned legislation, was the first program
of its kind in the United States. All communities hosting Olympic and Paralympic competitions
have pledged to rely on at least 60% renewable energy by 2030." If Cottonwood Heights did not
maintain the promise it made as one of those communities, it would be letting down the bid that

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for May 19, 2026            Approved: _ _ _ _ __

was put fo1ward, with the vast majority committing to 100% by that time. The City was one of
the communities in Utah that were ready for this. The bid goes on to state, "Rocky Mountain
Power has provided its enthusiastic support for future games in Utah and is the largest regulated
utility owner of wind power in the western United States." It mentions the community' s
c01mnitmcnt in three other areas.

For the Cottonwood Heights community at the base of the canyons to stay in the program would
reflect its commitment to those values. Mayor Bennion supported the URC for many reasons. It
is well thought out and includes guardrails. The program is a partnership with Rocky Mountain
Power overseen by the Public Service Commission, so guardrails are in place.

Utilities ("SLPCU"). Public Works Director, Matt Shipp reported that he drafted the resolution
after receiving a letter from SLPCU outlining what Tier 2 means. TI1e City cannot enforce water
restrictions, but he believed it would be advantageous to remind residents of the drought and show
the City's support for conservation.

Mr. Topham stated that if drought conditions worsened, the City Council could consider enacting
an ordinance prohibiting outside watering during certain hours of the day. However, because it is
not a water provider, the City does not have the ability to monitor usage.


Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026          Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                      1
Mayor Bennion noted that the resolution included the following language:

        1.      Standard Watering Schedule. Residents served by SLCPU or JVWCD are strongly
                encouraged to limit outdoor lawn irrigation to a maximum of twice per week

        2.      Private Irrigation Users. Residents on private irrigation systems are encouraged
                to strictly follow the specific guidance provided by their system's managers,
                recogni:.ling that these systems operate on a traditional "water turn" basis.

        3.      Smart Technology & Weather Awareness. City urges the use of "smart irrigation
                controllers" that adjust based on local weather data. Regardless of the schedule, no
                irrigation should occur during high rain events to prevent significant water waste.

        4.      Deep Watering and Trees. Residents should practice "deep watering" to encourage
                resilient root growth. Preservation of estahlished trees is a priority; trees should be
                watered directly to ensure they continue to provide shade and air filtration for the
                community.

        5.      Xeriscape and Plantings. City promotes transitioning to xeriscaping and drought-
                tolerant native plantings. Residents arc encouraged to utilize local landscape
                principles to design functional, water-wise landscapes.

expensive than he expected, but it is nice.

3.0     REPORTS

        3.1     Salt Lake County Presentation on County Budget. Services. and Key
                Priorities. Kara Trevino am/ Members of the Countv Council.

The above presentation took place after item 4.1.

Salt Lake County Council Chair, Aimee Winder Newton, introduced Legislative Affairs Director,
Kara Trevino. At-large County Council Memhcr, Suzanne Hanison, and District 6 County
Council Member, Dee Theodore introduced themselves.

County Council Member Newton rep01ted that they created a similar slide presentation for the
State Legislature and wanted to share that infonnation with cities. Salt Lake County historically

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026             Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                      2
served both regional and municipal roles for unincorporated areas of the County, and State statute
required that they have separate budgets for each role. There was a wave of incorporations in the
1990s, including Cottonwood Heights. In the early 2000s, several townships became cities. A bill
passed in 2024 required all unincorporated islands to be annexed or incorporated by July 2027,
after which the only remaining unincorporated areas will be in the canyons and the County could
focus solely on its regional role.

County Council Member Hanison reviewed a list of services the County provides:

   •        Three-quarters of the County's general fund is dedicated to public safety. They are
            responsible for the jail, prosecution, district attorney, indigent defense, criminal justice
            services, etc.
    e       The County Clerk runs both general and municipal elections.
    •       The Salt Lake County Health Department inspects restaurants, offers vaccination
            clinics, and addresses health issues to ensure that the pub1ic stays safe.
    •       Aging and Adult Services provides-Meals on Wheels for indigent elderly residents and
            offers a lunch program and activities at its senior centers. The federally mandated
            Meals on Wheels program delivers over 2,000 meals a day to residents~ many of whom
            are at severe risk for food ins ecmity.
    •       In Utah, counties are the desigmted hehavioral health authorities and receive state and
            federal funding for those programs. They had worked hard to increase services and
            were opening three group homes in 2026 to house citizens with severe mental illness.
    •       The Salt Palace Convention Center and Mountain Amc1ica Expo Center are both
            owned by Salt Lake County.
    •       Tourism and sales taxes in the County save each household approximately $1,900 per
            year in prope1ty taxes. Tourism dollars also fund the County's parks and recreation
            department, as well as cultural and arts facilities.
    •       The County maintains regional parks, trails, and open spaces and offers a free My
            County Rec Pass that allows all children under 18 years of age to access any County
            recreation center, ice rink, pool, or climbing wall.
    •       Salt Lake County has a nationally recognized, award-winning library system. Because
            Murray and Salt Lake City had their own library systems and in order to avoid douhle
            taxation, libraries are funded through a prope1ty tax levy in all other jurisdictions. All
            library expenses are funded through property taxes.
    •       The County also collects and distributes property taxes with a 97% collection rate .

County Council Member Newton reported that Salt Lake County was trying to limit its role to
services that should be regional. The County has a mayor-and-council form of government.
Mayor Wilson is the executive in charge of day-to-day operations. The County Cotmcil consists
ofnine memhers, three at-large and six representing districts. Independent officials are also elected
for specific County roles based on State requirements. The County had decided to dissolve its
justice court in 2027.

County Council Member Newton discussed the County budget and repm1ed that the State Auditor
required all pass-through taxes and Utal1 Transit Authority ("UTA") fLmds to be run through the

Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026             Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                      3
budget even though the County has no control over those monies. Although the budget appears to
be $2 billion, it is actually only $1.5 billion. Transient Room Tax ("TRT") and Tourism,
Recreation, Cultural and Convention ("TRCC") tax revenues can only be used for tourism-related
purposes; culture, arts, parks and recreation, and the convention centern are funded through those
taxes. The general fund is approximately $500 million, and those monies are used for core
government services, primarily public safety. Special revenue funds for services like libraries and
behavioral health are restricted to specific uses, and all expenses in those categories must he paid
through those discretionary funds. Enterprise funds like the go] f courses, landfill, and public works
funds are self-sustaining; fees collected for services cover all expenses.

A total of 74% of the County's general fund goes to public safety, including the jail, district
attorney, indigent legal services, and criminal justice. Due to Truth in Taxation, inflation does not
increase revenue. As a result, Salt Lake County increased property taxes approximately every five
years to catch up with inflation. A 15% increase was instituted in 2025. County Council Member
Newton reported that the last tax increase was in 2019, and the 15% increase did not match
inflation over the same period. They had contemplated moving to small yearly inflationary
increases to avoid large increases every five to six years. A sample property tax statement was
reviewed, indicating the portion of property taxes that are received by the County, and County
Council Member Newton noted that the more educated citizens arc on Truth in Taxation, the better.

In response to a question from County Council Member Newton, it was reported that Cottonwood
Heights had only done one property tax increase since its incorporation. Council Member Hyland
stated that she had heard feedback from residents indicating that they would prefer gradual
increases that can be more easily assimilated into their household budgets. The Truth in Taxation
process is difficult and meant to stymy cities. It was noted that West Valley has yearly inflationary
increases of 2.8% to 3%.

County Council Member Newton reported that Salt Lake County is one of only 42 counties in the
nation with a AAA bond rating, and a referendum to vote down a tax increase could affect that
rating. -~The Salt Lake County Council conducts regular stress testing of all departments, where
each agency must propose 3% in cuts to use as a budgeting tool.

County Council Member Theodore reviewed public safety needs. The County has two jails:
Oxbow, which holds approximately 360 inmates, and the main adult detention center, which holds
approximately 2,000 inmates. A top need was jail expansion as they were at capacity, and the
Oxbow facility was failing. Their plan was to expand the main facility. A GO bond for the project
failed, but they were committed to addressing the issue with a lease revenue bond. There were
also more mental health and substance use issues, as well as more people leaving the jail into
homelessness. The jail expansion would include increasing mental health treatment from 88 to
160 beds.

Council Member Binell thanked the County Council for safety improvements to Old Wasatch
Boulevard, as well as the work of the Board of Health and the regional planning depaitment.
County Council Member Theodore encouraged the City Council to reach out to them with any
concems.


Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026            Approved: _   _ _ _ __

        4.3     Public Safety Range Discussion - Police Chief. Robbv Russo.

Police Chiet: Robby Russo, reported that the Public Safety Training Range is a strategic
investment in officer readiness, regional partnerships, and community safety. The City's vision
was to partner with other agencies along the Wasatch Front, and they had obtained signed letters
of acknowledgment and appreciation from police chiefs in the Salt Lake Valley, Utah Highway
Patrol, the Canyons School Distiict, and others.

The project will provide a secure, modern, indoor fireanns range for the Cottonwood Heights
Police Department ("CH PD"), school guardians, and regional partners. Scenarios can be produced
on screen in a controlled environment so that, for example, an officer can be trained on how to
react to a car driving toward him while trying to flee, which will improve officer readiness and
response capabilities and may avoid a crisis in the future. The public safety asset was supported
through federal funding and designed with sound mitigation, security, and aesthetics in mind.

Chief Russo reviewed the financial aspects of the project as follows:

    •  A $2.1 million federal grant was received to purchase the range.
    •  The CHPD currently pays approximately $7,000 each year to rent outside ranges for
       training.
    • The proj ccted annual outside agency revenue was projected at a minimum of $62,000 based
       on rates for the Murray range. CHPD currently pays $500 per one-half day in rental fees,
      i nd the rate was increasing to $600.
    • Long-term maintenance costs were estimated at $17,500 per year to maintain the filtration
       system. Lead removal cost \Vas net zero as removal contractors credited the City for its
       value.

Independent sound testing for the Multiple interactive Learning Objectives ("MILO") range that
was provided by the manufachlrcr indicates that sound levels drop from 102 to 103 dB inside the
range to approximately 70 dB at 100 feet, which is comparable to common urban background
noise. The closest home to the proposed location is 140 feet away. Hearing and eye protection
are required inside the range. Chief Russo indicated that the ranges visited by the City Council
were not insulated, hut the City's range will have modem sound mitigation systems.

In response to a question from Council Member Hyland, Chief Russo confinned that there would
also be an eight-foot wall and landscaping sunounding the range, which will further reduce noise.

Chief Russo reported that a Certified Range Master is required during live-fire operations, but an
additional full-time cqui valcnt ("fTE") cmpl oyec would not be required, as each agency will bring


Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026         Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                      7
their own Range Master to conduct training in the facility. The range will be opened, closed, and
inspected by Cottonwood Heights' Range Masters as part of their nomial duties. There will also
be strict outside-agency agreements, including liability waivers and insurance requirements.
Routine visual inspections will include ventilation, backstops, lighting, and emergency systems.
The automated system will also ale1t the City of any issues.

TI1c range is constructed of steel and fully contained, and the manufacturer reported that they had
never had a round escape the unit. They have ranges inside a Bass Pro Shop and a police
department surrounded by cubicles, and the range will stop a .50 caliber round. Assistant Police
Chief, Chris McHugh, added that the highest caliber allowed in the range will be .223 for patrol
rifles. Other agencies carry .308 sniper rifles, but they arc not practical in a 25-yard pistol range.

In response to a question raised by Council Member Hi land, it was confinncd that the restriction
would be included in the agency agreement. Outside~agency Range Masters will also receive
training on the types of weapons and ammunition allowed in the range. Although it is rifle-rated
to a higher level, CHPD does not want rumor-piercing rounds shot inside the range because of the
possibility of damage. It will also be inspected after use to ensure that no damage has occurred.

In response to a question from Council vlcmbcr BiITell, Assistant Chief McHugh confinned that
the agency responsible for the damage will be responsible for repair costs. A visual inspection
will be conducted after use. The facility is also equipped with internal cameras, so any damage
found afler inspection can be determined.

An aerial map of the proposed location was displayed. Chief Russo reported that MILO chose the
location out of several options. It was also the City's prefened location as it is beside the Police
Department and can be easily secured.

Public Works Director, Matt Shipp reported that they considered locations all over the City, but
this w~s_::the best location for accessibility and security. It will be west of the Police Station in a
small -detention pond. Changes will be necessary, but it will have minimal impact on the
stonnwater system. Distance from the building and closest residence was maximized as much as
possible. The closest residence is approximately 140 feet from the facility. Placement will also
maximum open space available for K-9 training. The facility will be fully enclosed and accessed
through the Police Station. There will be a park strip area between the wall and parking lot and
larger landscaping area at the front, and plantings will add both aesthetic and sound barriers.

Council Member Hyland asked if the facility could be accessed through the exterior fence. It was
confinned that the only access will be through the Police Dcparhnent. Chief Russo reported that
everyone must check in prior to accessing the facility.

Regarding the K-9 training area, Chief Russo rep01ted that most training is conducted off leash,
and the area was cun-ently open. An added benefit of the wall is that it will create a secure training
area for K-9 units.

Chief Russo discussed the facility's expected lifespan. Paint and metal were designed to last for
40 years. They can be repaired or rehabilitated, and MILO will purchase the unit if the City decides
to end operations.

Council Member Newell shared two stories about the importance of de-escalation. As a teenager,
he attended school in another area of the city as part of a desegregation program. He passed a
police car on his way home from football practice and saw it turn around. His parents taught him
not to pull over until he reached home, so the officers chased him to his home. At 16, he was
pulled out of the car at gunpoint and accused of driving a stolen vehicle. It was his father' s car.
His neighbors were home and verified that infonnation. The officers then re-holstered their guns
and left without saying anything. In the second incident, he was a 19-year-old football player for
the University of Utah. He and six friends were pulled over, removed from the vehicle, and frisked.
The officers only stopped when they saw the university logos on their shirts.



Cottonwood Heights City Council Meeting Minutes for June 2, 2026           Approved: _ _ _ _ __

                                                      9
Simulation systems are required to help de-escalate situations and ensure that all officers are
trained in the proper way to engage, because those types of incidents still happen today. Without
proper training and understanding of how to de-escalate a situation, there will be problems. The
range will have a positive impact on Cottonwood Heights and many communities across the
Wasatch Front. The community asked for de-escalation training in 2020, and the City now has the
opportunity to provide it. Council Member Holton and Chief Russo had done an amazing job of
obtaining the funding and getting police chiefs excited about the facility. It is an imperative need
and will allow the City to become a leader in ensuring that civil and human rights are protected.

Council Member Newell remarked that Utah has an anned citizemy, which requires a different
level of engagement. There are guardians in the high schools, and people openly carrying in the
streets, and this type of training is needed. He is an advocate for the facility and hopes that his
points helped people understand the importance of educating police officers in a professional way
so they are able to police the community in a professiQnal way.

Council Member Holton stated that a murder-suicide occurred the previous week, and his wife
asked how officers know when they are walkin-g3{~to an active, violent situation. He told her that
he did not think they knew, and her response was-that she hoped they know what they're doing.
Training is c1itical. Things happened in the count1y fiv~ to six years ago that caused people to
demand better de-escalation training, and he hoped the community could see that the facility would
deliver on that request and ensure that Cottonwood Heights police officers are properly trained on
both using their weapon if necessary and de-escalation. He was grateful for Canyons School
District's support for the facility. State law requires guardians with firearms in schools, and they
need proper training.

Mayor Bennion stated that she visited the MILO facility with Chief Russo and Assistant Chief
McHugh. She agreed that there is a need for training, but was concerned about the site. The
original plan was to locate the facility near the Public Works complex and away from residential
areas and City Hall. Her husband is a retire □ Air Force physician who specialized in occupational
and environmental medicine, and his recommendation was that it be located 70 feet from
residential propc1iics for noise reduction and 400 feet for Iead. The person perfonning lead
removal does so in a hazmat suit, and there is a possibility of lead escaping into the environment
during removal. Because the City does not have a specific noise ordinance, it relies on County
standards. For a typical single-family residence, the maximum noise level is 60 dB during the day
and 50dB at night. She rec01mnended that the City conduct a study to detennine the actual noise
level outside of the gate and fence.

Mayor Bennion asked if the City could partner with another municipality to site the facility outside
of Cottonwood Heights. Chief Russo clarified that the facility was presented as a City asset that
would he shared regionally and would add great value to the City. They had no desire to seek
placement in another municipality. Mayor Bennion stated that City Manager, Jared Gerber, had
told her that a school site and other locations were considered, but there were not a lot of options
available. There was nothing in writing requiring the facility to be located within the City, and
they could potentially partner with another municipality to find an optional site that will not impact
a residential neighborhood. Chief Russo stated that it had been suggested, hut the Police
Department opposed it because the City would lose control of a valuable asset. He did not agree

under negative pressure. Filters are changed out inside and placed in bags in that same area, then
removed by the same company.
